The Hytönen-Vuorinen L p L^{p} conjecture for the Hilbert transform, with an extended energy side condition, when 4 3 < p < 4 \frac{4}{3}<p<4 and the measures share no point masses Thanks: † \dagger Research supported in part by a grant from the National Science and Engineering Research Council of Canada. Thanks: ‡ \ddagger B. D. Wick’s research is supported in part by National Science Foundation Grants DMS # 1800057, # 2054863, and # 2000510 and Australian Research Council – DP 220100285.
Eric T. Sawyer †, Brett D. Wick ‡

Abstract
. In the case 4 3 < p < 4 \frac{4}{3}<p<4 , and assuming σ \sigma and ω \omega are locally finite positive Borel measures on ℝ \mathbb{R} without common point masses, we prove variants of two conjectures of T. Hytönen and E. Vuorinen from 2018 on two weight testing theorems for the Hilbert transform on weighted L p spaces, but only in the presence of extended energy conditions. Namely, if we assume the extended energy condition ∫ ℝ ( ∑ W ∈ 𝕎 ( P ⁡ ( W , Φ F ​ f ​ σ ) ℓ ⁡ ( W ) ) 2 ​ | 𝖯 [ W ] ω | 2 ​ Z ) p 2 ​ 𝑑 ω ≲ ℰ p ℓ 2 , e ​ x ​ t ​ ( σ , ω ) p ​ ‖ f ‖ L p ​ ( σ ) p , \int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sum_{W\in\mathbb{W}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(W,\Phi_{F}f\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(W\right)}\right)^{2}\left|\mathsf{P}_{\left[W\right]}^{\omega}\right|^{2}Z\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}d\omega\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)^{p}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)}^{p}, for f ≥ 0 f\geq 0 , along with the dual extended energy condition, then the two weight norm inequality ‖ H σ ​ f ‖ L p ​ ( ω ) ≲ ‖ f ‖ L p ​ ( σ ) , 4 3 < p < 4 , \left\|H_{\sigma}f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)},\ \ \ \ \ \frac{4}{3}<p<4, holds (1) if and only if the global quadratic interval testing conditions hold, (2) if and only if the local quadratic interval testing, the quadratic Muckenhoupt, the quadratic weak boundedness conditions all hold. We also give a slight improvement of the second characterization by replacing the quadratic Muckenhoupt conditions with two smaller conditions.



1.
Introduction

Almost a century ago in 1927, M. Riesz
[
Rie
]
proved that the conjugate
function is bounded on
L^{p}\left(\mathbb{T}\right)
of the circle
\mathbb{T}
for all
1<p<\infty
. Forty-six years later in 1973, R. Hunt,
B. Muckenhoupt and R.L. Wheeden
[
HuMuWh
]
showed that the conjugate
function on
\mathbb{T}
, equivalently the Hilbert transform on the real
line
\mathbb{R}
, extends to a bounded operator on a weighted
space
L^{p}\left(w\right)
, i.e.

(1.1)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|Hf\left(x\right)\right|^{p}w\left(x\right)dx\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|f\left(x\right)\right|^{p}w\left(x\right)dx,\ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }f\in L^{p}\left(w\right),

if and only if the weight
w
satisfies the remarkable
A_{p}
condition of
Muckenhoupt
1
1

1

A function theoretic characterization is available in the case
p=2
. For
example, in 1960, H. Helson and G. Szegö
[
HeSz
]
showed that (
1.1
) holds for
p=2
if and only if
w=e^{u+Hv}
for some bounded
functions
u,v
with
\left\|u\right\|_{\infty}<\frac{\pi}{2}
.
,

\left(\frac{1}{\left|I\right|}\int_{I}w\left(x\right)dx\right)\left(\frac{1}{\left|I\right|}\int_{I}\left(\frac{1}{w\left(x\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}dx\right)^{p-1}\leq A_{p}\left(w\right),\ \ \ \ \ \text{for all intervals }I\subset\mathbb{R}.

However, an extension of the
A_{p}\left(w\right)
characterization, even
in the case
p=2
, to the setting of two weights has proven to be much more
difficult, especially in view of the exceptional beauty and utility of the
A_{p}\left(w\right)
condition, which has greatly spoiled expectations
2
2

2

The Helson-Szegö result was extended in 1983 to the two weight setting
for all
1<p<\infty
by M. Cotlar and C. Sadosky
[
CoSa
]
.
. Many
sufficient conditions involving ‘bumped-up’ variants of
A_{p}\left(w\right)
have been obtained by numerous authors over the ensuing years,
but while such conditions are in a sense ‘checkable’, they cannot
characterize the two weight inequality for the Hilbert transform due to its
instability, at least in the case
p=2
, see
[
AlLuSaUr 2
]
.

Instead, using the ‘testing condition’ approach originating with E. Sawyer

[
Saw
]
and G. David and J.-L. Journé
[
DaJo
]
, groundbreaking
strides were made in 2004 toward a characterization in the case
p=2
by F.
Nazarov, S. Treil and A. Volberg
[
NTV4
]
. Finally, in 2014, the
Nazarov-Treil-Volberg
T1
conjecture
[
Vol
]
on the boundedness of the
Hilbert transform from one weighted space
L^{2}\left(\sigma\right)
to
another
L^{2}\left(\omega\right)
, was settled affirmatively, when the
measures
\sigma,\omega
share no common point masses, in the two part
paper
[
LaSaShUr3
]
;
[
Lac
]
of M. Lacey, C.-Y. Shen, E. Sawyer, I.
Uriarte-Tuero; M. Lacey. Subsequently, T. Hytönen
[
Hyt
]
removed the
assumption of no common point masses. A number of modifications of the NTV
approach were required in this solution, including the use in
[
LaSaShUr3
]
of functional energy and the two weight inequalities for Poisson
integrals from
[
Saw3
]
, as well as M. Lacey’s use of a size condition,
an upside-down corona construction, and a recursion to bound the obstinate
stopping form in
[
Lac
]
, and the use in
[
Hyt
]
of two weight
inequalities for Poisson integrals with holes.

The testing approach in the case
p\neq 2
has proven even more challenging,
going back at least to the 2015 primer of M. Lacey
[
Lac2
, page 18]
- a
major source of difficulty being the resistance of known arguments to using
energy conditions when
p\neq 2
. Moreover, it was recently shown in M.
Alexis, J.-L. Luna-Garcia, E. Sawyer and I. Uriarte-Tuero
[
AlLuSaUr 2
]
,
that the usual scalar testing and Muckenhoupt conditions do not suffice for
boundedness of the Hilbert transform for any
p\neq 2
.

On the other hand, T. Hytönen and E. Vuorinen
[
HyVu
]
have made two
challenging conjectures regarding extensions to weighted
L^{p}
norms,
1<p<\infty
, that involve
quadratic
testing, Muckenhoupt, and weak
boundedness conditions, described in a moment.

The purpose of this paper is to prove two weaker variants of the conjectures
of Hytönen and Vuorinen in the special case that
\frac{4}{3}<p<4
and
the measures
\sigma
and
\omega
share no point masses, and to give a
slight improvement of the second variant in this setting, thus completing a
first step toward a two-weight
L^{p}
theory for the Hilbert transform. We
remark that there is only one place in the proof where the restriction
p<4

arises, namely in Lemma
38
that plays a supporting role in
bounding the
stopping form
at the end of the paper. When possible we
will state and prove our supporting results for
1<p<\infty
and general
measures, but for simplicity we assume in our theorems that the
\sigma

and
\omega
share no common point masses. Here are two open problems
weaker than the full conjectures of Hytönen and Vuorinen. Of course we
can ask for inclusion of the necessary extended energy conditions in these
two problems as well.

Problem 1
.

Do the conjectures of Hytönen and Vuorinen hold for measures
with

common point masses when
\frac{4}{3}<p<4
?

Problem 2
.

Do the conjectures of Hytönen and Vuorinen hold for measures
without
common point masses when
1<p<\infty
?

In order to state our theorems, we need a number of definitions. Let
\mu

be a positive locally finite Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathcal{D}

be a dyadic grid on
\mathbb{R}
, and let
\left\{\bigtriangleup_{Q}^{\mu}\right\}_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}
be the associated set of weighted Haar
projections on
L^{2}\left(\mu\right)
, see e.g.
[
NTV4
]
. In
particular
\bigtriangleup_{Q}^{\mu}f\left(x\right)=\left\langle f,h_{Q}^{\mu}\right\rangle_{\mu}h_{Q}^{\mu}\left(x\right)
where
\left\{h_{Q}^{\mu}\right\}_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}
is the associated
orthonormal Haar basis (that may include averages over infinite intervals of
finite
\mu
-measure). Denote the Hilbert transform
H
of a signed measure

\nu
defined by

H\nu\left(x\right)\equiv\mathop{\mathrm{p}v}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{y-x}d\nu\left(y\right),

and for
1<p<\infty
, consider the two weight norm inequality,

(1.2)

\left\|H_{\sigma}f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|H_{\sigma}f\right|^{p}d\omega\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|f\right|^{p}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}=\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)},

where
\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is the best
constant taken over all admissible truncations of the Hilbert transform, and
where
H_{\sigma}f\equiv H\left(f\sigma\right)
, see e.g.
[
SaShUr10
, subsubsection 1.2.1 on page 130]
for more detail on this
interpretation of the norm inequality that avoids consideration of principal
values (as pioneered by X. Tolsa). The following definitions are for the
most part from Hytönen and Vuorinen
[
HyVu
]
, except for the extended
energy conditions.

1.1.
Quadratic testing conditions

The
local scalar
(forward) interval

testing characteristic
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is defined as
the best constant in

(1.3)

\left\|\mathbf{1}_{I}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\leq\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left|I\right|_{\sigma}^{\frac{1}{p}},

and the
local quadratic
(forward) interval testing characteristic
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
, is
defined as the best constant in

(1.4)

\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\leq\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)},

taken over all sequences of intervals
\left\{I_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and all sequences of positive numbers
\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
. The dual scalar and quadratic interval testing
characteristics are obtained by interchanging
\sigma
and
\omega
, and
replacing
p
with
p^{\prime}
.

The
global scalar
interval

testing characteristic
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is defined as the
best constant in

(1.5)

\left\|H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\leq\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left|I\right|_{\sigma}^{\frac{1}{p}},

and the
global quadratic
testing characteristic
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is defined
as the best constant in

(1.6)

\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{i}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\leq\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)},

taken over all sequences of intervals
\left\{I_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and all sequences of positive numbers
\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and similarly for the dual scalar and global
characteristics
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p^{\prime}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
and
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)
. Note that by Khintchine’s inequality, the
global quadratic condition (
1.6
) can be interpreted as ‘average
testing’ over finite linear spans of indicators of intervals,

\mathbb{E}_{\pm}\left\|H_{\sigma}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pm a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\lesssim\mathbb{E}_{\pm}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{M}\pm a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)},

which in the case
p=2
reduces to the global
scalar
testing
condition (
1.5
).

1.2.
Extended energy conditions

Given any interval
F\in\mathcal{D}
, we define the
\left(r,\varepsilon\right)
-Whitney
collection
\mathcal{M}_{\left(r,\varepsilon\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{d}eep}}\left(F\right)
of
F
to be the set of dyadic
subintervals
W\subset F
that are maximal with respect to the property that

W\subset_{r,\varepsilon}F
. Clearly the intervals in
\mathcal{M}_{\left(r,\varepsilon\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{d}eep}}\left(F\right)
form a pairwise
disjoint decomposition of
F
.where the extended energy characteristic
\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is
the best constant in the inequality,

The extended energy characteristic
\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is defined to be the smallest constant in
the inequality

(1.7)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{W\in\mathcal{M}_{\left(r,\varepsilon\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{d}eep}}\left(F\right)\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(W,\Phi_{F}f\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(W\right)}\right)^{2}\left|\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\cap\mathcal{D}\left[W\right]}^{\omega}\right|^{2}Z\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}d\omega\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)^{p}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)}^{p},\ \ \ \ \ \text{for }f\geq 0,

where
\mathcal{F}
is the collection of stopping times associated with
f
,
and

\Phi_{F}f\equiv\mathbb{E}_{F}^{\sigma}f+f_{F}=\mathbb{E}_{F}^{\sigma}f+\sum_{I:\ I\supsetneqq F}\mathbf{1}_{I_{F}}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f.

Remark 3
.

Note that (
1.7
) is a
positive
inequality, and like the
quadratic Muckenhoupt characteristics defined in the next subsection,
depends only on the measures
\sigma
and
\omega
, and not on any
singular operator such as the Hilbert transform. In the special case
p=2
,
the characteristic
\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is controlled by testing and Muckenhoupt characteristics,
even the logically larger functional energy characteristic, see e.g.
[
LaSaShUr3
]
and
[
SaShUr7
]
, but we are unable to extend all of the
Poisson inequality arguments used there when
p\neq 2
.

There is a similar dual quadratic extended energy characteristic
\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt},\ast}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)

in which
\sigma
and
\omega
are interchanged, the index
p
is replaced
by its conjugate index
p^{\prime}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\sum_{W\in\mathcal{M}_{\left(r,\varepsilon\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{d}eep}}\left(G\right)\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}\left(G\right)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(W,\Phi_{G}g\omega\right)}{\ell\left(W\right)}\right)^{2}\left|\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}\left(G\right)\cap\mathcal{D}\left[W\right]}^{\sigma}\right|^{2}Z\right)^{\frac{p^{\prime}}{2}}d\sigma\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt},\ast}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)^{p^{\prime}}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)}^{p^{\prime}},\ \ \ \ \ \text{for }g\geq 0,

where
\mathcal{G}
is the collection of stopping times associated with
g
.

Remark 4
.

Note that the Whitney intervals
W
, in the above definition of
the quadratic extended energy characteristic
\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
, are restricted to lie in the
corona
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)
. This departure from the
analogous definitions in
[
LaSaShUr3
]
,
[
SaShUr7
]
and elsewhere in
the literature, constitutes an important simplification of the argument in
the case
p=2
, and is largely responsible for our successful control of the
far form when
p\neq 2
. The key point here is that any
W\in\mathcal{D}

lies in
\mathcal{M}_{\left(r,\varepsilon\right)-\mathop{\mathrm{d}eep}}\left(F\right)\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)
for at most one
F\in\mathcal{F}
.

1.3.
Main theorems

Now we can state our first main result, which in the case of no common point
masses, extends the results in
[
LaSaShUr3
]
,
[
Lac
]
and
[
Hyt
]
to

\frac{4}{3}<p<4
by replacing
2
with
p
at the expense of introducing
quadratic testing characteristics, and answers in the affirmative the first
conjecture in
[
HyVu
]
in this range, provided we include the necessary
extended energy conditions in the characterizing conditions. The case of
doubling measures was obtained in
[
SaWi
]
, even for vector Riesz
transforms in higher dimensions and
1<p<\infty
, and the case
p=2
was
done earlier in
[
AlSaUr
]
. The case of
dyadic shifts
was resolved
much earlier for
1<p<\infty
and general measures in
[
Vuo
]
.

Theorem 5

(first conjecture of Hytönen and Vuorinen)
.

Suppose
\frac{4}{3}<p<4
, and that
\sigma
and
\omega

are locally finite positive Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}
without common
point masses. Suppose also that the extended energy characteristics
\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
and

\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt},\ast}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)
are finite. Then under these side conditions, the two
weight norm inequality (
1.2
) holds
if and only if
the
global quadratic interval testing conditions (
1.6
) hold. Moreover, we
have

\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\lesssim\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathfrak{T}_{H,p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)+\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt},\ast}\left(\omega,\sigma\right).

In order to replace global quadratic testing with the smaller local
quadratic testing, we must include additional quadratic Muckenhoupt
characteristics and a quadratic weak boundedness characteristic, which we
now define.

1.3.1.
Quadratic and scalar tailed Muckenhoupt conditions

The
global
quadratic Muckenhoupt characteristic
\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
of Hytönen and Vuorinen is defined as the best constant in

(1.8)

\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{i}}\frac{f_{i}\left(x\right)}{\left|x-c_{i}\right|}d\sigma\left(x\right)\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\leq\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}f_{i}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)},

taken over all sequences of intervals
\left\{I_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
with centers
\left\{c_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and all sequences
of functions
\left\{f_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
. There is also the
usual dual characteristic defined by interchanging
\sigma
and
\omega
,
and replacing
p
by
p^{\prime}
.

We now introduce three smaller Muckenhoupt characteristics whose use we will
track throughout the proof - only the disjoint form requires the triple
Muckenhoupt characteristic, and only the outer form bound requires the
kernel Muckenhoupt characteristic, while the offset Muckenhoupt
characteristic suffices elsewhere.

The smaller
offset
quadratic Muckenhoupt characteristic
A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{o}ffset}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is defined as the best
constant in

(1.9)

\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|a_{i}\frac{\left|I_{i}^{\ast}\right|_{\sigma}}{\left|I_{i}^{\ast}\right|}\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\leq A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{o}ffset}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|a_{i}\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}^{\ast}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)},

where
I_{i}^{\ast}
is taken over the finitely many dyadic intervals
I_{i}^{\ast}
disjoint from
I_{i}
and such that
\ell\left(I_{i}^{\ast}\right)=\ell\left(I_{i}\right)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{d}ist}\left(I_{i}^{\ast},I_{i}\right)\leq r\ell\left(I_{i}\right)
, and all sequences numbers
a_{i}
, where
r
is the goodness constant from
[
NTV4
]
and
[
LaSaShUr3
]
- see also the section on preliminaries. There is again the
usual dual characteristic defined by interchanging
\sigma
and
\omega
,
and replacing
p
by
p^{\prime}
.

There is also an intermediate
triple
quadratic Muckenhoupt
characteristic
A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
defined as the best constant in

(1.10)

\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\int_{3I_{i}\setminus I_{i}}\frac{f_{i}\left(x\right)}{\left|x-c_{i}\right|}d\sigma\left(x\right)\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\leq A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}f_{i}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)},

taken over all sequences of intervals
\left\{I_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
with centers
\left\{c_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and all sequences
of functions
\left\{f_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}f_{i}\subset 3I_{i}\setminus I_{i}
, as well as the dual such characteristic

A_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)
.

Next, there is the
scalar tailed
Muckenhoupt characteristic defined
by,

(1.11)

\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\approx\sup_{I\text{ an
interval}}\left(\frac{1}{\left|I\right|}\int\left(\frac{\ell\left(I\right)}{\ell\left(I\right)+\mathop{\mathrm{d}ist}\left(x,I\right)}\right)^{p}d\omega\left(x\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\ \left(\frac{\left|I\right|_{\sigma}}{\left|I\right|}\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}.

We have

\displaystyle A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{o}ffset}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\lesssim\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right),

but there is no obvious relationship we can see between
\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
and
A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{o}ffset}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
.

1.3.2.
Quadratic weak boundedness property

The
quadratic
weak boundedness characteristic
\mathcal{WBP}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is defined as the best
constant in

(1.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}a_{i}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\left(x\right)b_{i}\mathbf{1}_{J_{i}}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{WBP}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)}\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|b_{i}\mathbf{1}_{J_{i}}\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)},

taken over all sequences
\left\{I_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
\left\{J_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}

and
\left\{b_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
of intervals and numbers
respectively where
J_{i}
denotes any interval
adjacent
to
I_{i}

with comparable side length up to a factor of
2^{r}
, where here
adjacent
means the closures of
I_{i}
and
J_{i}
have nonempty
intersection while the interiors of
I_{i}
and
J_{i}
have empty
intersection. Clearly, this characteristic is symmetric,
\mathcal{WBP}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)=\mathcal{WBP}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)
, and is used only in bounding the
comparable form.

Remark 6
.

We observe that in the proofs of our main theorems, we use only a slightly
weaker form of these characteristics, namely where the sequences
\left\{I_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, etc., used above are restricted to lie in a
fixed dyadic grid
\mathcal{D}
, provided that we require uniform control
over all grids
\mathcal{D}
.

Now we can state our second main theorem, which again, in the case of
measures without common point masses, extends the results in
[
LaSaShUr3
]
,
[
Lac
]
and
[
Hyt
]
to
\frac{4}{3}<p<4
by replacing
2
with
p
,
but using only a local quadratic testing characteristic, at the expense of
introducing additional quadratic Muckenhoupt and weak boundedness
characteristics - of course the extended energy characteristics are included
in the characterizing conditions. The case of doubling measures was again
obtained in
[
SaWi
]
, even for general Calderón-Zygmund operators
and
1<p<\infty
, and with the
scalar
local testing condition (
1.3
) in place of quadratic local testing, and the case
p=2
is in
[
AlSaUr
]
. As already mentioned, it was shown in
[
AlLuSaUr 2
]
that one cannot replace all quadratic conditions with their
scalar analogues for any
p\neq 2
. The case of
dyadic shifts
was
again resolved for general measures and all
1<p<\infty
in
[
Vuo
]
.

The following theorem provides a slight improvement over our variant
(including extended energy conditions) of the second conjecture of Hytönen and Vuorinen in the range
\frac{4}{3}<p<4
when the measures share no
point masses, in that the global Muckenhoupt characteristics are replaced by
the smaller triple Muckenhoupt characteristics and scalar tailed Muckenhoupt
characteristics.

Theorem 7

(second conjecture of Hytönen and Vuorinen)
.

Suppose
\frac{4}{3}<p<4
, and that
\sigma
and
\omega
are
locally finite positive Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}
without common point
masses. Suppose also that the extended energy characteristics
\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
and
\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt},\ast}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)
are finite. Then under these side conditions, the two weight norm
inequality (
1.2
) holds
if and only if
the local quadratic
interval testing conditions (
1.4
) hold, the triple
quadratic Muckenhoupt conditions (
1.10
) hold, the scalar tailed
Muckenhouopt conditions (
1.11
) hold, and the quadratic
weak boundedness property (
1.12
) holds. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathfrak{T}_{H,p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)+A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+A_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathcal{A}_{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)+\mathcal{WBP}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt},\ast}\left(\omega,\sigma\right).

1.3.3.
Dual half-forms

In order to better understand the extended energy characteristics, write the
bilinear form
\left\langle H_{\sigma}f,g\right\rangle_{\omega}
as a sum
of two ‘dual’ half-forms,

\left\langle H_{\sigma}f,g\right\rangle_{\omega}=\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I,J\in\mathcal{D}\\
\ell\left(J\right)\leq\ell\left(I\right)\end{subarray}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I,J\in\mathcal{D}\\
\ell\left(J\right)>\ell\left(I\right)\end{subarray}}\right\}\left\langle H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f,\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\right\rangle_{\omega}\equiv\mathsf{B}^{\leq}\left(f,g\right)+\mathsf{B}^{>}\left(f,g\right),

determined by the relative size of the side lengths
\ell\left(I\right),\ell\left(J\right)
of
I
and
J
. Denote the norms of these bilinear
half-forms by
\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{\leq}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
and

\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{>}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
respectively, i.e.
\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{\leq}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is the best
constant in the inequality,

\left|\mathsf{B}^{\leq}\left(f,g\right)\right|\leq\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{\leq}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)}\ ,

and similarly for
\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{>}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
.
Note that
\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\leq\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{\leq}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{>}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
. We can actually
characterize
the sum of the
characteristics
\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{\leq}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{>}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
.

Theorem 8

(characterization of sum of dual half-form norms)
.

Suppose
\frac{4}{3}<p<4
, and that
\sigma
and
\omega
are
locally finite positive Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}
without common point
masses. There are the following characterizations of the sum of the norms of
the dual half forms in terms of testing, Muckenhoupt, weak boundedness and
extended energy characteristics,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{\leq}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{>}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \approx\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathfrak{T}_{H,p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)+\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt},\ast}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \approx\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathfrak{T}_{H,p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)+A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+A_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathcal{A}_{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)+\mathcal{WBP}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt},\ast}\left(\omega,\sigma\right).

1.4.
Guide for the reader

Here we emphasize the most basic ideas used to handle the case
p\neq 2
of
the main theorems, given that the case
p=2
was solved back in 2014 using
orthonormal weighted Haar bases in a Hilbert space, something not available
when
p\neq 2
. Neverthess, a large portion of the
p=2
proof from 2014
finds its way into the arguments here, and the reader is encouraged to have
at least some of the papers
[
LaSaShUr3
]
,
[
Lac
]
,
[
Hyt
]
and

[
Saw7
]
at hand while reading this one. To get started, we use the idea
of Hytönen and Vuorinen to test, not the
scalar
inequality for
H

over indicators of intervals, but rather to test the
\ell^{2}
-valued
extension, which has the same norm, over sequences of indicators of
intervals (times constants). In order to use these and other quadratic
testing conditions effectively, we follow
[
Saw7
, Subsection 2.1]
and
decompose our bilinear form

\left\langle H_{\sigma}f,g\right\rangle_{\omega}=\sum_{I,J\in\mathcal{D}}\left\langle H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f,\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\right\rangle_{\omega}

into subforms, of which a typical example can be written as

\mathsf{B}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(f,g\right)=\sum_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\left\langle H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f,\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\right\rangle_{\omega}=\sum_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\left\langle\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f,\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\right\rangle_{\omega}

for some subset of pairs
\mathcal{P}
of
\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}
.
We then proceed with the inequalities of Cauchy-Schwarz in
\ell^{2}
, and Hölder in
L^{p}\left(\omega\right)
, to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{B}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(f,g\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\sum_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f\left(x\right)\ \bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\left(x\right)\right\}\ d\omega\left(x\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\sum_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f\left(x\right)\right|^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\left(x\right)\right|^{2}}\ d\omega\left(x\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\sqrt{\sum_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f\left(x\right)\right|^{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\left\|\sqrt{\sum_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\left(x\right)\right|^{2}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\left|\left\{\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f\right\}_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\right|_{\ell^{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\left\|\left|\left\{\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\right\}_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}\right|_{\ell^{2}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)}.

At this point, Burkholder’s theorem on martingale differences yields a
square function estimate that can be used to show that the second factor is
controlled by
\left\|g\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)}
provided the pairs
\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}
have only
a bounded number of
I^{\prime}s
paired with a given
J
. In order to
handle the first factor we need to manipulate the sequence
\left\{\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f\right\}_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}
so as to apply one of the
quadratic hypotheses. The entire difficulty with this approach lies in
appropriately decomposing the original bilinear form, and in finding
vector-valued manipulations so that the two goals can be simultaneously
achieved.

In order to proceed further, we need the fundamental insight of Nazarov,
Treil and Volberg that we may restrict our attention to functions with Haar
support consisting of
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
intervals, which enjoy crucial geometric
decay properties. We also follow the blueprints of work in the case
p=2
by
Hytönen, Lacey, Sawyer, Shen, Uriarte-Tuero and Wick, in particular that
of the work in
[
Saw7
]
and
[
Lac
]
, with a couple of exceptions.
There is no appeal to weighted Poisson inequalities. There is no explicit
use of the size condition in this proof. Instead, the ‘magical’ property
\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{x}=-\frac{1}{x^{2}}
for
x\neq 0
, of the convolution
kernel
\frac{1}{x}
of the Hilbert transform is directly used in the proof
of the
L^{p}
-Stopping Child Lemma, and an elaborate stopping energy
characteristic finishes control of the stopping form. Thus our approach
provides a new proof in the case
p=2
as well, in view of the fact that the
extended energy can be controlled by testing and Muckenhoupt in this case.

Then most of the decompositions into subsubforms go by the name of
corona decompositions
, in which a collection of
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
stopping
times (by stopping times we simply mean a collection of dyadic intervals,
whether or not they were chosen by some stopping criterion) is chosen so
that various features of the inner products are controlled in the coronas
lying ‘between’ the stopping times. These features include the averages of
f
over
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
intervals in a corona and the total amount of ‘scalar

p
-energy’ within a corona. Of course there is a price to pay for
arranging control of these special features, and we are able to pay it only
if there are Carleson type conditions that can be derived from the stopping
time criteria.

All of this information must then be encoded in the sequences
\left\{\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f\right\}_{\left(I,J\right)\in\mathcal{P}}
in such a way that it can
be exploited, and this requires different approaches in each separate
instance. Examples of this can be found in the ensuing sections where the
major forms are analyzed using a variety of tools. In particular we need the
following preliminary tools from Section 3 below.

(1)

Traditional two weight tools
: the
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
/
\mathop{\mathrm{b}ad}

interval technology and Poisson inequalities in Lemma
14
of Nazarov, Treil and Volberg (from
[
NTV4
, Subsetion 4.1]

and
[
Vol
]
); the monotonicity equivalence in Lemma
11

(from
[
LaSaShUr3
, Section 4]
), including the simple new Lemma
13
; and standard properties of Carleson measures.

(2)

L^{p}
specific two weight tools
: A new vector-valued Theorem
17
for Carleson measures with
1<p<\infty
; the square
function Theorem
18
for corona martingale differences that
uses Burkholder’s theorem (from
[
Bur1
]
and
[
Bur2
]
); extension (
3.16
) of the vector-valued maximal inequalities of Fefferman and Stein

[
FeSt
]
to the dyadic setting of a general measure (observed by J.-L.
Luna-Garcia), as well as the useful Lemma
23
; and finally a
Corona Martingale Comparison Principle in Proposition
34
that can be
viewed as a variant of the comparison principles for martingale differences
in J. Zinn
[
Zin
]
.

In particular we point to the challenges of the
far
and
stopping
forms in Sections 6, 8 and 9, whose analysis takes up much of this paper,
and moreover requires the restriction to
p<4
for handling the stopping
form.

The main tool used to bound the
far
form is the Intertwining
Proposition, which controls the
far
form by a new
extended

energy, which is shown to be necessary for the norm inequality
3
3

3

It is interesting to note that even in the case
p=2
there is no short
direct proof of the necessity of extended energy for the norm inequality.
The traditional approach is to go through the two weight Poisson inequality
and its equivalent Poisson testing conditions, which is a long and difficult
road. Here is this paper we derive necessity using most of the estimates for
the entire proof - again a long and difficult road.
. See Remark
4

for more detail on extended energy.

There are three main tools used to bound the stopping form, namely a dual
tree decomposion generalizing Lacey’s ‘upside down’ corona construction, a
martingale difference comparison principle that delivers a form of
‘orthogonality’, and an
L^{p}
-Stopping Child Lemma. At least three major
obstacles appear, with the first arising from the fact that we no longer
have additivity of Hilbert space projections that played a prominent role in
the ‘upside down’ corona construction of Lacey in
[
Lac
, page 8]
. The
second arises from the lack of a counterpart to the Quasi-Orthogonality
Argument in
[
Lac
, page 6]
when
p\neq 2
, which leads to an elaborate
extension of the stopping form, and as Lacey writes in his primer
[
Lac2
, page
4]
, ”This argument (
referring to the case
p=2
in

[
Lac
, page 6]
) relies heavily on the Hilbertian structure of the
question.” Possibly the most significant obstacle is the failure of our
methods to obtain a suitable extension of the decay in Lemma
38
in Subsection 9.6 to
p\geq 4
, thus limiting our control of the
stopping form to
p<4
.

In Section 2 we treat necessity of the quadratic conditions and defer the
necessity of extended energy to Section 9, then the preliminaries are
treated in Section 3, followed by the sufficiency proof of the two main
theorems in Sections 4 (comparable and disjoint forms), 5 (neighbour form),
6 (far form), 7 (paraproduct form), 8 (stopping form, which requires the
restriction
p<4
) and then necessity of extended energy in Section 9.
Concluding remarks are made in Section 10 on the restriction to
\frac{4}{3}<p<4
in controlling the stopping form.

2.
Necessity of testing conditions

To derive the necessity of the local quadratic testing, quadratic
Muckenhoupt and quadratic weak boundedness conditions, we will need a
special case of the classical Hilbert space valued extension of a bounded
operator from one
L^{p}
space to another, see e.g.
[
Gra
, Theorem 4.5.1]
. Suppose
T
is bounded from
L^{p}\left(\mathbb{R};\sigma\right)

to
L^{p}\left(\mathbb{R};\omega\right)
,
0<p<\infty
, and for
\mathbf{f}=\left\{f_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
, define

T\mathbf{f}\equiv\left\{Tf_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{\infty}.

Then
T
extends to an operator bounded from
L^{p}\left(\ell^{2};\sigma\right)
to
L^{p}\left(\ell^{2};\omega\right)
with the same norm,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|T\mathbf{f}\left(x\right)\right|_{\ell^{2}}^{p}d\omega\left(x\right)\leq\left\|T\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)\rightarrow L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}^{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbf{f}\left(x\right)\right|_{\ell^{2}}^{p}d\sigma\left(x\right),

which written out in full becomes

(2.1)

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left|Tf_{j}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}}\right)^{p}d\omega\left(x\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\left\|T\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)\rightarrow L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left|f_{j}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}}\right)^{p}d\sigma\left(x\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

2.1.
Necessity of quadratic testing and offset
A_{p}
, and WBP

We can use the vector-valued inequality (
2.1
) with
T=H_{\sigma}

to obtain the necessity of the global quadratic testing inequality (
1.6
) for the boundedness of
H
from
L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)
to
L^{p}\left(\omega\right)
. Indeed, we simply set
f_{j}\equiv a_{j}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}
in (
2.1
) to obtain the global
quadratic testing inequality (
1.6
). Then we simply note the pointwise
inequality

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)\left(x\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|a_{i}\right|^{2}\left|H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}\mathbf{,}

to obtain the local version (
1.4
). Altogether we have,

\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\leq\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\lesssim\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right).

The quadratic offset
A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{o}ffset}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
characteristic is controlled by the global quadratic
testing characteristic
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lobal}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
using the pointwise estimate
\left|H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}^{\ast}}\left(x\right)\right|\gtrsim\frac{\left|I_{i}^{\ast}\right|_{\sigma}}{\left|I_{i}\right|}
for
x\in I_{i}
, and the quadratic weak boundedness condition also
follows from global quadratic testing,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}a_{i}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\left(x\right)b_{i}\mathbf{1}_{J_{i}}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)\right|\leq\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{i}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\omega\right)}\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(b_{i}\mathbf{1}_{J_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \lesssim\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)}\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(b_{i}\mathbf{1}_{J_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)}.

Finally, it is claimed without proof in
[
HyVu
]
that finiteness of the
global quadratic Muckenhoupt characteristic
\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is necessary for the norm
inequality. However, the reader can now easily provide a proof modeled on
that for the offset condition above, after writing
f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{i}}=f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{L_{i}}+f_{i}\mathbf{1}_{R_{i}}
, where
L_{i}
and
R_{i}
are the left and right hand components of
\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{i}
respectively
4
4

4

We thank Ignacio Uriarte-Tuero for discussions on this matter.
. Of course,
the triple quadratic Muckenhoupt characteristic
A_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
, and the scalar tailed characteristic
\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
, are controlled by the global
quadratic Muckenhoupt characteristic
\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
. Finally, it is well known in the case

p=2
that the scalar tailed characteristic
\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
is controlled by the scalar global testing characteristic
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\mathop{\mathrm{g}lob}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
, and the
same proof works for
p\neq 2
.

2.2.
Necessity of extended energy for boundedness of the dual half
forms

Recall the extended energy characteristic
\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
and its dual
\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)

given in inequality (
1.7
).

Theorem 9
.

Suppose
\sigma
and
\omega
are locally finite
positive Borel measures on the real line
\mathbb{R}
. For
1<p<\infty
we
have

\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\lesssim\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{\leq}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\text{ and }\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\lesssim\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{>}\left(\sigma,\omega\right).

We defer the proof to Section
9
below. In the case
p=2
,
each of the extended energy characteristics are controlled by the global
quadratic testing characteristics
[
LaSaShUr3
]
, but when
p\neq 2
, we
are unable to show even that
\mathcal{E}_{p}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathcal{E}_{p^{\prime}}^{\ell^{2},\mathop{\mathrm{e}xt}}\left(\omega,\sigma\right)\lesssim\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
. Thus when
p=2
, we have
\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)\approx\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{\leq}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)+\mathfrak{N}_{H,p}^{>}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
, but it remains an open question whether or not this persists for any
p\neq 2
.

3.
Preliminaries

We will need the
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}/\mathop{\mathrm{b}ad}
technology of Nazarov, Treil and
Volberg, a Monotonicity Lemma, a Poisson Decay Lemma, an estimate on sums of
Poisson kernels, a
p
-energy reversal inequality, several properties of
Carleson measures, bounds for square functions using Burkholder’s martingale
transform theorem and Kintchine’s expectation theorem, an extension of
Fefferman-Stein vector-valued inequalitites to the dyadic maximal function
with arbitrary measures, and finally a Corona Martingale Comparison
Principle in order to control the stopping form in the final section of the
paper - an important new feature of the proof.

Recall the formula

(3.1)

f=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f,\ \ \ \ \ \text{where }\bigtriangleup_{Q}^{\sigma}f=\left\langle f,h_{Q}^{\sigma}\right\rangle_{\sigma}h_{Q}^{\sigma}\text{ and }h_{Q}^{\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left|Q\right|_{\sigma}}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\left|Q_{-}\right|_{\sigma}}{\left|Q_{+}\right|_{\sigma}}}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{+}}-\sqrt{\frac{\left|Q_{+}\right|_{\sigma}}{\left|Q_{-}\right|_{\sigma}}}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{-}}\right),

and where
Q_{\pm}
denote the right and left hand children of the interval

Q
.

3.1.
Good/bad intervals and functions

For the purposes of this paper, an
interval

I=\left[a,b\right)

will be taken to be closed on the left and open on the right, unless
otherwise stated. We recall the definition of a
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
dyadic
interval from
[
NTV4
, Subsection 4.1]
, see also
[
LaSaUr2
]
. We say
that a dyadic interval
J
is
\left(r,\varepsilon\right)
-
deeply
embedded
in a dyadic interval
K
, or simply
r
-deeply embedded
in
K
, which we write as
J\subset_{r}K
, when
J\subset K
and both

(3.2)

\displaystyle\ell\left(J\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{-r}\ell\left(K\right),

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{d}ist}\left(J,\partial K\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\ell\left(J\right)^{\varepsilon}\ell\left(K\right)^{1-\varepsilon}.

Definition 10
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\varepsilon<1
.

(1)

A dyadic interval
J
is
\left(r,\varepsilon\right)
-
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
, or simply
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
, if for
every
dyadic superinterval
I
, it is the case that
either

J
has side length at least
2^{-r}

times that of
I
,
or

J\subset_{r}I
is
\left(r,\varepsilon\right)
-deeply embedded in
I
.

(2)

A dyadic interval
J
is
\mathop{\mathrm{c}hild}
-
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
if
J

and its two dyadic children
J_{\pm}
are
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
.

(3)

Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{c}hild}}
the set of
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{c}hild}
-
\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}
intervals respectively.

It is shown in
[
NTV4
, Theorem 4.1 on page 15]
that for parameters
r,\varepsilon
sufficiently large and small respectively, the boundedness
of the Hilbert transform
H_{\sigma}:L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)\rightarrow L^{p}\left(\omega\right)
can be reduced to testing the bilinear
inequality

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{\sigma}f\left(x\right)g\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)\right|\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\omega\right)}\ ,

uniformly over all dyadic grids
\mathcal{D},
and all functions
f=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{D}}\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f\in L^{p}\left(\sigma\right)\cap L^{2}\left(\sigma\right)
and
g=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{D}}\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\in L^{p}\left(\omega\right)\cap L^{2}\left(\omega\right)
whose Haar supports
\left\{I\in\mathcal{D}:\bigtriangleup_{I}^{\sigma}f\neq 0\right\}
are
\left\{J\in\mathcal{D}:\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\neq 0\right\}
are contained in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{c}hild}}
5
5

5

Only the case
\mathcal{D}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}
and
p=2
is mentioned in
[
NTV4
, Theorem 4.1]
, but the proof extends readily to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{c}hild}}
and
1<p<\infty
.
. The parameters
r,\varepsilon

will be fixed sufficiently large and small respectively later in the proof.

3.2.
Poisson and Monotonicity Lemmas

For any interval
J
with center
c_{J}
, and any finite measure
\nu
,
define the Poisson integral,

\mathrm{P}\left(J,\nu\right)\equiv\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\ell\left(J\right)}{\left(\ell\left(J\right)+\left|y-c_{J}\right|\right)^{2}}d\nu\left(y\right).

Lemma 11

(Monotonicity Lemma
[
LaSaShUr3
, Section 4]
)
.

Fix a locally finite positive Borel measure
\omega
.
Let
J\
be a cube in
\mathcal{D}
. Let
\nu
be a positive measure
supported in
\mathbb{R}\setminus 2J
. Let
H
be the Hilbert transform.
Then for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we have the monotonicity principle,

\left|\left\langle H\nu,h_{J}^{\omega}\right\rangle_{\omega}\right|\approx\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\nu\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\left|\int_{J}\left(x-\beta\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)\right|=\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\nu\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\left|\left\langle Z-\beta,h_{J}^{\omega}\right\rangle_{\omega}\right|,

where
Z\left(x\right)=x
is the identity function on the real line.

Due to the importance of this result for the Hilbert transform, we repeat
the short proof here.

Proof.

With
c_{J}
equal to the center of
\ J
, and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we
have that
\left(x-c_{J}\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)
doesn’t
change sign on
J
by (
3.1
) and so,

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle H\nu,h_{J}^{\omega}\right\rangle_{\omega}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{J}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus 2J}\frac{1}{y-x}d\nu\left(y\right)\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{J}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus 2J}\frac{1}{y-x}-\frac{1}{y-c_{J}}d\nu\left(y\right)\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{J}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus 2J}\frac{x-c_{J}}{\left(y-x\right)\left(y-c_{J}\right)}d\nu\left(y\right)\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{J}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus 2J}\frac{\ell\left(J\right)}{\left|\left(y-x\right)\left(y-c_{J}\right)\right|}d\nu\left(y\right)\right)\left|\frac{x-c_{J}}{\ell\left(J\right)}h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\right|d\omega\left(x\right)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\left(J,\nu\right)\int_{J}\left|\frac{x-c_{J}}{\ell\left(J\right)}h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\right|d\omega\left(x\right),

and using

\displaystyle\int_{J}\left|\frac{x-c_{J}}{\ell\left(J\right)}h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\right|d\omega\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\ell\left(J\right)}\int_{J}\left(x-c_{J}\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\ell\left(J\right)}\int_{J}\left(x-\beta\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right),

we obtain

\left|\left\langle H\nu,h_{J}^{\omega}\right\rangle_{\omega}\right|\approx\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\nu\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\left|\int_{J}\left(x-\beta\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(x\right)\right|=\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\nu\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\left|\left\langle Z,h_{J}^{\omega}\right\rangle_{\omega}\right|.

Here is a
pointwise
corollary of the Monotonicity Lemma
11
, that estimates a Haar projection of
H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)
.

Corollary 12
.

For
J,K\in\mathcal{D}
with
2J
disjoint from
K
, we
have the pointwise estimate,

\left|\Delta_{J}^{\omega}H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(x\right)\right|\approx\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\left|\Delta_{J}^{\omega}Z\left(x\right)\right|\leq\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\mathbf{1}_{J}\left(x\right).

Proof.

The Monotonicity Lemma
11
yields

\left|\Delta_{J}^{\omega}H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(x\right)\right|=\left|\left\langle H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right),h_{J}^{\omega}\right\rangle_{\omega}\right|\left|h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\right|\approx\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\left|\left\langle Z,h_{J}^{\omega}\right\rangle_{\omega}\right|\left|h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\right|,

and then the following calculation completes the proof of the corollary,

\displaystyle\left|\Delta_{J}^{\omega}Z\left(x\right)\right|=\left|\Delta_{J}^{\omega}\left(Z-c_{J}\right)\left(x\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|E_{J_{-}}^{\omega}\left(Z-c_{J}\right)-E_{J}^{\omega}\left(Z-c_{J}\right)\right|\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right)+\left|E_{J_{+}}^{\omega}\left(Z-c_{J}\right)-E_{J}^{\omega}\left(Z-c_{J}\right)\right|\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left[\ell\left(J\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right)+\ell\left(J\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right)\right]=2\ell\left(J\right)\mathbf{1}_{J}\left(x\right).

The corollary applies to separated intervals
J,K
and we now show that the
upper bound in the corollary holds more generally for
disjoint

intervals
J
and
K
, including in particular adjacent intervals.

Lemma 13
.

For
J\in\mathcal{D}
that is disjoint from
K\in\mathcal{D}
, we have the pointwise estimate,

\left|\Delta_{J}^{\omega}H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(x\right)\right|\lesssim\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\mathbf{1}_{J}\left(x\right).

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Delta_{J}^{\omega}H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(x\right)=\left\langle H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right),h_{J}^{\omega}\right\rangle_{\omega}h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)=\int_{J}\left[H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(y\right)-\gamma\right]h_{J}^{\omega}\left(y\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)d\omega\left(y\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{J}\left[H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(y\right)-\gamma\right]h_{J}^{\omega}\left(y\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\left\{\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right)+\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right)\right\}d\omega\left(y\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{J}\left[H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(y\right)-\gamma\right]h_{J}^{\omega}\left(y\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\left\{\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right)+\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right)\right\}d\omega\left(y\right)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle A+B+C+D.

Hence using the formula (
3.1
) for the Haar function above,

\displaystyle h_{J}^{\omega}\left(y\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|J\right|_{\omega}}\frac{\left|J_{+}\right|_{\omega}}{\left|J_{-}\right|_{\omega}}\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right),

\displaystyle h_{J}^{\omega}\left(y\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|J\right|_{\omega}}\frac{\left|J_{-}\right|_{\omega}}{\left|J_{+}\right|_{\omega}}\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right),

\displaystyle h_{J}^{\omega}\left(y\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\left|J\right|_{\omega}}\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right),

\displaystyle h_{J}^{\omega}\left(y\right)h_{J}^{\omega}\left(x\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\left|J\right|_{\omega}}\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(y\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right).

Then we have with
\gamma=H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(c_{J}\right)
,

\displaystyle\left|A\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{J}\left[H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(y\right)-\gamma\right]\frac{1}{\left|J\right|_{\omega}}\frac{\left|J_{+}\right|_{\omega}}{\left|J_{-}\right|_{\omega}}\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(y\right)d\omega\left(y\right)\right|\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right)\lesssim\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right),

\displaystyle\left|B\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{J}\left[H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(y\right)-\gamma\right]\frac{1}{\left|J\right|_{\omega}}\frac{\left|J_{-}\right|_{\omega}}{\left|J_{+}\right|_{\omega}}\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(y\right)d\omega\left(y\right)\right|\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right)\lesssim\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right),

\displaystyle\left|C\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{J}\left[H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(y\right)-\gamma\right]\frac{1}{\left|J\right|_{\omega}}\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(y\right)d\omega\left(y\right)\right|\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right)\lesssim\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(x\right),

\displaystyle\left|D\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{J}\left[H\left(\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\left(y\right)-\gamma\right]\frac{1}{\left|J\right|_{\omega}}\mathbf{1}_{J_{+}}\left(y\right)d\omega\left(y\right)\right|\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right)\lesssim\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{K}\sigma\right)\mathbf{1}_{J_{-}}\left(x\right).

We will need the following critical Poisson Decay Lemma of Nazarov, Treil
and Volberg from
[
Vol
]
.

Lemma 14

(Poisson Decay Lemma)
.

Suppose
J\subset I\subset K
are dyadic intervals
and that
d\left(J,\partial I\right)>2\ell\left(J\right)^{\varepsilon}\ell\left(I\right)^{1-\varepsilon}
for some
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
. Then for any locally finite positive Borel measure
\mu
we have

(3.3)

\mathrm{P}(J,\mu\mathbf{1}_{K\setminus I})\lesssim\left(\frac{\ell\left(J\right)}{\ell\left(I\right)}\right)^{1-2\varepsilon}\mathrm{P}(I,\mu\mathbf{1}_{K\setminus I}).

There is an extension of the pointwise inequality (
3.6
) to a
variant involving ‘absolute’ projections

\left|\mathsf{P}_{\Lambda}^{\omega}\right|g\left(x\right)\equiv\sqrt{\sum_{J\in\Lambda}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}g\left(x\right)\right|^{2}},

where
\Lambda\subset\mathcal{D}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}\left[I\right]
.

Lemma 15
.

Suppose
I,F\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}
with
I\subset F
, and let
\Lambda\subset\mathcal{D}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}\left[I\right]
. Then
with
\mathcal{W}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(I\right)
denoting
the maximal good subintervals of
I
whose triples are contained in
I
, and
with
\Lambda\left[K\right]\equiv\Lambda\cap\mathcal{D}\left[K\right]
,

(3.4)

\sqrt{\sum_{K\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(I\right)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(K,\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(K\right)}\right)^{2}\left(\left|\mathsf{P}_{\Lambda\left[K\right]}^{\omega}\right|Z\left(x\right)\right)^{2}}\approx\left|\mathsf{P}_{\Lambda}^{\omega}\right|H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\left(x\right).

Proof.

Recall from the pointwise montonicity principle in Corollary
12

above that,

\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\left(x\right)\right|\approx\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}Z\left(x\right)\right|,\ \ \ \ \ \text{for }2J\cap I=\emptyset\ .

Moreover, we also have
\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\approx\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(K,\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(K\right)}
for
K\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(I\right)
, and so noting
J\subset K\subset I
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{P}_{\Lambda}^{\omega}\right|H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\left(x\right)^{2}=\sum_{K\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(I\right)}\sum_{J\in\Lambda\left[K\right]}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}\approx\sum_{K\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(I\right)}\sum_{J\in\Lambda\left[K\right]}\left(\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(J,\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(J\right)}\right)^{2}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}Z\left(x\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sum_{K\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(I\right)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(K,\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(K\right)}\right)^{2}\sum_{J\in\Lambda\left[K\right]}\left|\bigtriangleup_{J}^{\omega}Z\left(x\right)\right|^{2}=\sum_{K\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathop{\mathrm{g}ood}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{t}rip}}\left(I\right)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(K,\mathbf{1}_{F\setminus I}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(K\right)}\right)^{2}\left|\mathsf{P}_{\Lambda\left[K\right]}^{\omega}\right|Z\left(x\right)^{2}.

3.3.
Reversal of the Mononotonicity Lemma

Our proof will use a stopping energy inequality in order to bound the
stopping form. But first we note an easy pointwise precursor to this
inequality. Let
\left\{I_{r}\right\}_{r=1}^{\infty}
be a pairwise
disjoint decomposition of an interval
I
into subintervals
I_{r}
. Then
for
x,y\in I_{r}\subset I
with
y<x
:

(3.5)

\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(I_{r},\mathbf{1}_{I\setminus I_{r}}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(I_{r}\right)}\left[x-y\right]\leq 2\left[H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I\setminus I_{r}}\left(x\right)-H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I\setminus I_{r}}\left(y\right)\right].

Fix
r
for the moment and set
I_{r}=\left[a,b\right]
. We now fix
c\in\left(a,b\right)
such that
6
6

6

See
[
LaSaUr2
]
for the easy modifications in the case when no such
c

exists.

\left|\left[a,c\right]\right|_{\omega}=\left|\left[c,b\right]\right|_{\omega}=\frac{1}{2}\left|\left[a,b\right]\right|_{\omega}
, and set
I_{r,-}\equiv\left[a,c\right]

and
I_{r,+}\equiv\left[c,b\right]
. Then for
x\in I_{r,+}
, average
both sides of the displayed inequality in the variable
y
over
I_{r,-}

with respect to
\omega
to obtain

(3.6)

\mathbf{1}_{I_{r,+}}\left(x\right)\frac{\mathrm{P}\left(I_{r},\mathbf{1}_{I\setminus I_{r}}\sigma\right)}{\ell\left(I_{r}\right)}\left[x-E_{I_{r,-}}^{\omega}Z\right]\leq 2\mathbf{1}_{I_{r,+}}\left(x\right)\left[H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I\setminus I_{r}}\left(x\right)-E_{I_{r,-}}^{\omega}\left(H_{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{I\setminus I_{r}}\right)\right].

Remark 16
.

Using (
3.6
), one can control the
p
-Poisson-energy
characteristic
\mathcal{E}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
by the scalar
testing characteristic
\mathfrak{T}_{H,p}^{\mathop{\mathrm{l}oc}}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
and Muckehhoupt characteristic
\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
. See
[
LaSaUr2
, Proposition 2.11 ]
for the case
p=2
,
and below for the case
p\neq 2
. However, the Muckehhoupt characteristic
\mathcal{A}_{p}\left(\sigma,\omega\right)
can be dropped, see Lemma
27
below.

3.4.
Carleson measures

Here we recall some simple properties of Carleson measures and conditions
from
[
LaSaShUr3
]
and
[
SaShUr7
]
, where the case
p=2
was
considered - the general case
1<p<\infty
is similar. Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{D}
and let the corona
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)
consist of all intervals contained in
F
that are not contained
in any smaller interval from
\mathcal{F}
. We say that the triple
\left(C_{0},\mathcal{F},\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\right)
constitutes
stopping
data
for a function
f\in L_{loc}^{1}\left(\mu\right)
if,

(
1
):
\ \ E_{I}^{\mu}\left|f\right|\leq\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)
for all
I\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)
and
F\in\mathcal{F}
,

(
2
):
\ \ \sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}:\ F^{\prime}\subset F}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}\leq C_{0}\left|F\right|_{\mu}
for all
F\in\mathcal{F}
,

(
3
):
\ \ \sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)^{p}\left|F\right|_{\mu}\mathbf{\leq}C_{0}^{p}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mu\right)}^{p}
,

(
4
):
\ \ \alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\leq\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F^{\prime}\right)
whenever
F^{\prime},F\in\mathcal{F}
with
F^{\prime}\subset F
.

Moreover there is the following useful consequence of (2) and (3) that says
the sequence
\left\{\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\mathbf{1}_{F}\right\}_{F\in\mathcal{F}}
has a
quasiorthogonal
property
relative to
f
with a constant
C_{0}^{\prime}
depending only on
C_{0}
:

(3.7)

\left\|\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\mathbf{1}_{F}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mu\right)}^{p}\leq C_{0}^{\prime}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mu\right)}^{p}.

Indeed, this follows easily from the fact that the Carleson condition (2)
implies a geometric decay in levels of the tree
\mathcal{F}
, namely that
there are positive constants
\delta
and
C_{\delta}
, depending on
C_{0}
, such that if
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right)
denotes the set of
n^{th}
generation children of
F
in
\mathcal{F}
,

(3.8)

\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right)}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}\leq C_{\delta}2^{-\delta n}\left|F\right|_{\mu},\ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }n\geq 0\text{ and }F\in\mathcal{F}.

To see this well known result of Carleson, let
\beta_{k}\left(F\right)\equiv\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(k\right)}\left(F\right)}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}
and note that
\beta_{k+1}\left(F\right)\leq\beta_{k}\left(F\right)
implies that
for any integer
N\geq C_{0}
, we have

\left(N+1\right)\beta_{N}\left(F\right)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N}\beta_{k}\left(F\right)\leq C_{0}\left|F\right|_{\mu}\ ,

and hence

\beta_{N}\left(F\right)\leq\frac{C_{0}}{N+1}\left|F\right|_{\mu}<\frac{1}{2}\left|F\right|_{\mu}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \text{for }F\in\mathcal{F}\text{ and }N=\left[2C_{0}\right]_{0}.

It follows by iteration that

\beta_{\ell N}\left(F\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\left(\ell-1\right)N}\left(F\right)\leq...\leq\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\beta_{0}\left(F\right)=\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\left|F\right|_{\mu},\ \ \ \ \ \ell=0,1,2,...

and so given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, choose
\ell
such that
\ell N\leq n<\left(\ell+1\right)N
, and note that

\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right)}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}=\beta_{n}\left(F\right)\leq\beta_{\ell N}\left(F\right)\leq\frac{1}{2^{\frac{n}{N}}}\left|F\right|_{\mu}=2^{-\frac{n}{\left[2C_{0}\right]}}\left|F\right|_{\mu}=2^{-n\delta}\left|F\right|_{\mu}\ ,

which proves the geometric decay (
3.8
). Inequality (
3.7
) will actually be proved in the course of proving the next theorem. With a
slight abuse of notation we will also refer to inequality (3) above as a
quasiorthogonality property.

The following inequality will be used in controlling both the far form and
the paraproduct form later on. Its proof uses a technique introduced in
[
SaWi
, see the proof of the bound for the paraproduct form]
, that goes back
decades in other situations, see e.g.
[
BoBo
]
. Actually, the proof here
predates that in
[
SaWi
]
, but this result was not needed in the setting
of doubling measures, and so was left out of
[
SaWi
]
.

Theorem 17
.

Suppose that the triple
\left(C_{0},\mathcal{F},\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\right)
constitutes
stopping data
for a
function
f\in L_{loc}^{1}\left(\mu\right)
, and for
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, set

\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}^{\kappa}\left(x\right)\equiv\left\{\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\mathbf{1}_{F^{\kappa}}\left(x\right)\right\}_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\ \text{where }F^{\kappa}\equiv\bigcup_{G\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(\kappa\right)}\left(F\right)}G\text{ }.

Then for
1<p<\infty
,

(3.9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}^{\kappa}\left(x\right)\right|_{\ell^{2}}^{p}d\mu\left(x\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{F^{\kappa}}\left(x\right)\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}d\mu\left(x\right)\leq C_{\delta}2^{-\delta\kappa}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)^{p}\left|F\right|_{\mu}\ ,

where
\delta>0
is the constant in (
3.8
). The inequality can
be reversed for
\kappa=0
and
2\leq p<\infty
.

Proof.

We begin with the observation that
F_{1}^{\kappa}\subset F_{2}^{\kappa}

whenever
F_{1}\subset F_{2}
, which will be used repeatedly below without
further mention. We now claim that for
1<p<\infty
,

(3.10)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{F^{\kappa}}\left(x\right)\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}d\mu\left(x\right)\leq C_{\delta}2^{-\delta\kappa}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)^{p}\left|F\right|_{\mu}.

Indeed, for
1<p\leq 2
(and even for
0<p\leq 2
), the inequality follows
from the trivial inequality
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\ell^{q}}\leq\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\ell^{1}}
for
0<q\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{F^{\kappa}}\left(x\right)\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}d\mu\left(x\right)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\right|^{p}\mathbf{1}_{F^{\kappa}}\left(x\right)d\mu\left(x\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ =\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)^{p}\left|F^{\kappa}\right|_{\mu}\leq C_{\delta}2^{-\delta\kappa}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)^{p}\left|F\right|_{\mu},

where
\delta>0
is the geometric decay in generations exponent in (
3.8
).

Now we turn to the case
p\geq 2
. When
p=2m
is an even positive integer,
we will set

\mathcal{F}_{\ast}^{2m}\equiv\left\{\left(F_{1},...,F_{2m}\right)\in\mathcal{F}\times...\times\mathcal{F}:F_{i}\subset F_{j}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq j\leq 2m\text{, and }F_{i}=F_{i+1}\text{ for all odd }i\right\},

and then by symmetry we can arrange the intervals below in nondecreasing
order to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\mathbf{1}_{F^{\kappa}}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}d\mu\left(x\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\mathbf{1}_{F^{\kappa}}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}\right)^{m}d\mu\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{\left(F_{1},...,F_{2m}\right)\in\mathcal{F}^{2m}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)...\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2m}\right)\mathbf{1}_{F_{1}^{\kappa}\cap...\cap F_{2m}^{\kappa}}d\mu\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{\left(F_{1},...,F_{2m}\right)\in\mathcal{F}_{\ast}^{2m}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)...\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2m}\right)\mathbf{1}_{F_{1}^{\kappa}\cap...\cap F_{2m}^{\kappa}}d\mu\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{m}\sum_{\left(F_{1},...,F_{2m}\right)\in\mathcal{F}_{\ast}^{2m}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)...\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2m}\right)\left|F_{1}^{\kappa}\right|_{\mu}=C_{m}\mathop{\mathrm{I}nt}^{\kappa}\left(m\right),

where from the geometric decay in (
3.8
), we obtain

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{I}nt}^{\kappa}\left(m\right)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{\left(F_{1},...,F_{2m}\right)\in\mathcal{F}_{\ast}^{2m}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)...\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2m}\right)\left|F_{1}^{\kappa}\right|_{\mu}\lesssim 2^{-\delta\kappa}\mathop{\mathrm{I}nt}\left(m\right),

\displaystyle\text{where }\mathop{\mathrm{I}nt}\left(m\right)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{\left(F_{1},...,F_{2m}\right)\in\mathcal{F}_{\ast}^{2m}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)...\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2m}\right)\left|F_{1}\right|_{\mu}.

We now set about showing that

\mathop{\mathrm{I}nt}\left(m\right)\lesssim\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\right|^{2m}\left|F\right|_{\mu}\ .

For this, we first prove (
3.7
) in order to outline the main idea.
Using the geometric decay in (
3.8
) once more we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right):\ }\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F^{\prime}\right)\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F^{\prime}\right)^{2}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}}C_{\delta}2^{-\delta n}\sqrt{\left|F\right|_{\mu}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\delta}\sqrt{\left|F\right|_{\mu}}\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{-\delta n}\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F^{\prime}\right)^{2}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}},

and hence that

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\left\{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F^{\prime}\right)\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\sqrt{\left|F\right|_{\mu}}\sqrt{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{-\delta n}\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F^{\prime}\right)^{2}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)^{2}\left|F\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{-\delta n}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n\right)}\left(F\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F^{\prime}\right)^{2}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mu\right)}\left(\sum_{F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F^{\prime}\right)^{2}\left|F^{\prime}\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mu\right)}^{2}.

This proves (
3.7
) since
\left\|\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F\right)\mathbf{1}_{F}\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mu\right)}^{2}
is dominated by twice the left hand side above.

We now adapt this last argument to apply to (
3.11
). For example, in the
case
m=2
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{I}nt}\left(2\right)=\sum_{F_{4}\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{4}\right)\sum_{F_{3}\subset F_{4}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{3}\right)\sum_{F_{2}\subset F_{3}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2}\right)\sum_{F_{1}\subset F_{2}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)\left|F_{1}\right|_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{F_{4}\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{4}\right)\left(\sum_{n_{3}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{F_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{3}\right)}\left(F_{4}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{3}\right)\left(\sum_{n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{F_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{2}\right)}\left(F_{3}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2}\right)\left(\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{F_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{1}\right)}\left(F_{2}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)\left|F_{1}\right|_{\mu}\right)\right)\right)

which is at most (we continue to write
m
in place of
2
until the very
end of the argument)

\displaystyle C_{\delta}\sum_{n_{3}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{F_{4}\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{4}\right)\sum_{F_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{3}\right)}\left(F_{4}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \times\sum_{F_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{2}\right)}\left(F_{3}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2}\right)\left(2^{-\delta n_{1}}\left|F_{2}\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{2m-1}{2m}}\left(\sum_{F_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{1}\right)}\left(F_{2}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)^{2m}\left|F_{1}\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{1}{2m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\delta}\sum_{n_{3}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}2^{-\delta\frac{2m-1}{2m}n_{1}}\sum_{F_{4}\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{4}\right)\sum_{F_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{3}\right)}\left(F_{4}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \times\sum_{F_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{2}\right)}\left(F_{3}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2}\right)\left|F_{2}\right|_{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2m}}\left(\sum_{F_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{1}\right)}\left(F_{2}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)^{2m}\left|F_{1}\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{1}{2m}}\left|F_{2}\right|_{\mu}^{1-\frac{2}{2m}},

which is in turn dominated by

\displaystyle C_{\delta}\sum_{n_{3}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}2^{-\delta\frac{2m-1}{2m}n_{1}}\sum_{F_{4}\in\mathcal{F}}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{4}\right)\sum_{F_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{3}\right)}\left(F_{4}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(\sum_{F_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{2}\right)}\left(F_{3}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{2}\right)^{2m}\left|F_{2}\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{1}{2m}}\left(\sum_{F_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{2}\right)}\left(F_{3}\right)}\sum_{F_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{1}\right)}\left(F_{2}\right)}\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\left(F_{1}\right)^{2m}\left|F_{1}\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{1}{2m}}\left(2^{-\delta n_{2}}\left|F_{3}\right|_{\mu}\right)^{\frac{2m-2}{2m}},

where in the last line we have applied Hölder’s inequality with
exponents
\left(2m,2m,\frac{2m}{2m-2}\right)
, and then used that
\sum_{F_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\left(n_{2}\right)}\left(F_{3}\right)}\left|F_{2}\right|_{\mu}\leq C_{\delta}2^{-\delta n_{2}}\left|F_{3}\right|_{\mu}
.

Continuing in this way, we dominate the sum above by

\displaystyle\lesssim
